        function CustomEvent ( event, params ) {
            params = params || { bubbles: false, cancelable: false, detail: null };
            var evt = document.createEvent( 'CustomEvent' );
            evt.initCustomEvent( event, params.bubbles, params.cancelable, params.detail );
            return evt;
        }

        CustomEvent.prototype = window.Event.prototype;

        window.CustomEvent = CustomEvent;
    })();
</script>

                }),

                    this.registered.push(value);
                },

                ready: function () {
                    if (this.registered.length === this.scripts.length) {
                        this.registered.forEach(function (fn) {
                            if (typeof fn === 'function') {

                    } else {
                        d.head.appendChild(s);
                    }
                    ++this.index;
                },

                    var s = d.createElement('script');
                    s.id = 'js-position' + (this.index + 1);
                    s.setAttribute('data-test', script.test);

                        s.defer = 'defer';

                            if (script.noinit) {
                                loader.register(true);
                            }
                            if (d.readyState === 'interactive' || d.readyState === 'complete') {
                                loader.ready();
                            }
                        };
                    } else {
                        s.async = 'async';
                    }
                    s.src = script.src;
                    return s;
                },

    <script type="application/ld+json">{"mainEntity":{"headline":"Overcoming thermostability challenges in mRNA–lipid nanoparticle systems with piperidine-based ionizable lipids","description":"Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) have emerged as promising platforms for efficient in vivo mRNA delivery owing to advancements in ionizable lipids. However, maintaining the thermostability of mRNA/LNP systems remains challenging. While the importance of only a small amount of lipid impurities on mRNA inactivation is clear, a fundamental solution has not yet been proposed. In this study, we investigate an approach to limit the generation of aldehyde impurities that react with mRNA nucleosides through the chemical engineering of lipids. We demonstrated that piperidine-based lipids improve the long-term storage stability of mRNA/LNPs at refrigeration temperature as a liquid formulation. High-performance liquid chromatography analysis and additional lipid synthesis revealed that amine moieties of ionizable lipids play a vital role in limiting reactive aldehyde generation, mRNA–lipid adduct formation, and loss of mRNA function during mRNA/LNP storage. These findings highlight the importance of lipid design and help enhance the shelf-life of mRNA/LNP systems.     <meta name="dc.description" content="Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) have emerged as promising platforms for efficient in vivo mRNA delivery owing to advancements in ionizable lipids. However, maintaining the thermostability of mRNA/LNP systems remains challenging. While the importance of only a small amount of lipid impurities on mRNA inactivation is clear, a fundamental solution has not yet been proposed. In this study, we investigate an approach to limit the generation of aldehyde impurities that react with mRNA nucleosides through the chemical engineering of lipids. We demonstrated that piperidine-based lipids improve the long-term storage stability of mRNA/LNPs at refrigeration temperature as a liquid formulation. High-performance liquid chromatography analysis and additional lipid synthesis revealed that amine moieties of ionizable lipids play a vital role in limiting reactive aldehyde generation, mRNA&#8211;lipid adduct formation, and loss of mRNA function during mRNA/LNP storage. These findings highlight the importance of lipid design and help enhance the shelf-life of mRNA/LNP systems. Piperidine-based ionizable lipids avoid the formation of mRNA&#8211;lipid adduct that is responsible for the loss-of-function of mRNA and contribute to the long-term storage stability of mRNA/LNPs as a wet preparation."/>

    <meta name="description" content="Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) have emerged as promising platforms for efficient in vivo mRNA delivery owing to advancements in ionizable lipids. However, maintaining the thermostability of mRNA/LNP systems remains challenging. While the importance of only a small amount of lipid impurities on mRNA inactivation is clear, a fundamental solution has not yet been proposed. In this study, we investigate an approach to limit the generation of aldehyde impurities that react with mRNA nucleosides through the chemical engineering of lipids. These findings highlight the importance of lipid design and help enhance the shelf-life of mRNA/LNP systems. Piperidine-based ionizable lipids avoid the formation of mRNA&#8211;lipid adduct that is responsible for the loss-of-function of mRNA and contribute to the long-term storage stability of mRNA/LNPs as a wet preparation."/>

Taguchi</a><sup class="u-js-hide"><a href="#Aff1">1</a></sup>, </li><li class="c-article-author-list__item c-article-author-list__item--hide-small-screen"><a data-test="author-name" data-track="click" data-track-action="open author" data-track-label="link" data-track-index="3_9" data-track-context="researcher popup with no profile" href="#auth-Sachiko-Sakamoto-Aff1" data-author-popup="auth-Sachiko-Sakamoto-Aff1" data-author-search="Sakamoto, Sachiko">Sachiko Sakamoto</a><sup class="u-js-hide"><a href="#Aff1">1</a></sup>, </li><li class="c-article-author-list__item c-article-author-list__item--hide-small-screen"><a data-test="author-name" data-track="click" data-track-action="open author" data-track-label="link" data-track-index="4_9" data-track-context="researcher popup with no profile" href="#auth-Ayaka-Otsu-Aff1" data-author-popup="auth-Ayaka-Otsu-Aff1" data-author-search="Otsu, Ayaka">Ayaka Otsu</a><sup data-track-action="open author" data-track-label="link" data-track-index="5_9" data-track-context="researcher popup with no profile" href="#auth-Yoshiki-Maeda-Aff1" data-author-popup="auth-Yoshiki-Maeda-Aff1" data-author-search="Maeda, Yoshiki">Yoshiki Maeda</a><sup class="u-js-hide"><a href="#Aff1">1</a></sup>, </li><li class="c-article-author-list__item c-article-author-list__item--hide-small-screen"><a data-test="author-name" data-track="click" data-track-action="open author" data-track-label="link" data-track-index="6_9" data-track-action="open author" data-track-label="link" data-track-index="7_9" data-track-context="researcher popup with no profile" href="#auth-Arimichi-Okazaki-Aff1" data-author-popup="auth-Arimichi-Okazaki-Aff1" data-author-search="Okazaki, Arimichi">Arimichi Okazaki</a><sup class="u-js-hide"><a href="#Aff1">1</a></sup>, </li><li class="c-article-author-list__item c-article-author-list__item--hide-small-screen"><a data-test="author-name" data-track="click" data-track-action="open author" data-track-label="link" data-track-index="8_9" data-track-context="researcher popup with no profile" href="#auth-Hideyoshi-Harashima-Aff2" data-author-popup="auth-Hideyoshi-Harashima-Aff2" data-author-search="Harashima, Hideyoshi">Hideyoshi Harashima</a><span class="u-js-hide"> 
            <a class="js-orcid" href="https://orcid.org/0000-0002-1568-9547"><span class="u-visually-hidden">ORCID: </span>orcid.org/0000-0002-1568-9547</a></span><sup class="u-js-hide"><a href="#Aff2">2</a></sup> &amp; </li><li class="c-article-author-list__show-more" aria-label="Show all 9 authors for this article" title="Show all 9 authors for this article">…</li><li class="c-article-author-list__item"><a data-test="author-name" data-track="click" data-track-action="open author" data-track-label="link" data-track-index="9_9" data-track-context="researcher popup with no profile" href="#auth-Yusuke-Sato-Aff2" data-author-popup="auth-Yusuke-Sato-Aff2" data-author-search="Sato, Yusuke" data-corresp-id="c2">Yusuke Sato<svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-mail-medium"></use></svg></a><span class="u-js-hide"> 
            <a class="js-orcid" href="https://orcid.org/0000-0003-0913-7815"><span class="u-visually-hidden">ORCID: </span>orcid.org/0000-0003-0913-7815</a></span><sup class="u-js-hide"><a href="#Aff2">2</a></sup> </li></ul><button aria-expanded="false" class="c-article-author-list__button"><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-down-medium"></use></svg><span>Show authors</span></button>

        <div class="c-article-body">
            <section aria-labelledby="Abs1" data-title="Abstract" lang="en"><div class="c-article-section" id="Abs1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Abs1">Abstract</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Abs1-content"><p>Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) have emerged as promising platforms for efficient in vivo mRNA delivery owing to advancements in ionizable lipids. However, maintaining the thermostability of mRNA/LNP systems remains challenging. While the importance of only a small amount of lipid impurities on mRNA inactivation is clear, a fundamental solution has not yet been proposed. In this study, we investigate an approach to limit the generation of aldehyde impurities that react with mRNA nucleosides through the chemical engineering of lipids. We demonstrated that piperidine-based lipids improve the long-term storage stability of mRNA/LNPs at refrigeration temperature as a liquid formulation. High-performance liquid chromatography analysis and additional lipid synthesis revealed that amine moieties of ionizable lipids play a vital role in limiting reactive aldehyde generation, mRNA–lipid adduct formation, and loss of mRNA function during mRNA/LNP storage. These findings highlight the importance of lipid design and help enhance the shelf-life of mRNA/LNP systems.</p></div></div></section>

                <div class="main-content" >
                    
                        <section data-title="Introduction"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec1-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec1">Introduction</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec1-content"><p>mRNA has recently gained increasing importance and popularity because of its numerous advantages, including the ability to encode specific proteins and the potential for rapid development and production, such as the marketed mRNA-based vaccines developed against SARS-CoV-2<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 1" title="Baden, L. R. et al. Efficacy and safety of the mRNA-1273 SARS-CoV-2 vaccine. N. Engl. J. Med. 384, 403–416 (2021)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR1" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e450">1</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 2" title="Polack, F. P. et al. Safety and efficacy of the BNT162b2 mRNA Covid-19 vaccine. N. Engl. J. Med. 383, 2603–2615 (2020)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR2" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e453">2</a></sup>.</p><p>Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) are widely employed for in vivo delivery of mRNA; they protect the mRNA payload from degradation by nucleases and facilitate their delivery to target cells<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 3" title="Cullis, P. R. &amp; Hope, M. J. Lipid nanoparticle systems for enabling gene therapies. Mol. Ther. 25, 1467–1475 (2017)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR3" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e460">3</a></sup>. LNPs typically consist of various components, such as ionizable lipids, cholesterol, phospholipids, and polyethylene glycol (PEG)-conjugated lipids. Among these components, ionizable lipids contain a positively charged amino group that interacts with the negatively charged phosphate backbone of mRNA, thereby playing a critical role in encapsulating the mRNA and promoting its escape from the endosome<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Cornebise, M. et al. Discovery of a novel amino lipid that improves lipid nanoparticle performance through specific interactions with mRNA. Adv. Funct. Mater. 32, 2106727 (2022)." href="#ref-CR4" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e464">4</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Lam, K. et al. Unsaturated, trialkyl ionizable lipids are versatile lipid-nanoparticle components for therapeutic and vaccine applications. Adv. Mater. 35, e2209624 (2023)." href="#ref-CR5" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e464_1">5</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 6" title="Han, X. et al. An ionizable lipid toolbox for RNA delivery. Nat. Commun. 12, 7233 (2021)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR6" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e467">6</a></sup>. The evolution of ionizable lipids has enabled efficient in vivo mRNA delivery and broadened their applications in cancer vaccines, protein replacement therapy, and genome editing<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 7" title="Rohner, E., Yang, R., Foo, K. S., Goedel, A. &amp; Chien, K. R. Unlocking the promise of mRNA therapeutics. Nat. Biotechnol. 40, 1586–1600 (2022)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR7" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e471">7</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 8" title="Gillmore, J. D. et al. CRISPR-Cas9 in vivo gene editing for transthyretin amyloidosis. N. Engl. J. Med. 385, 493–502 (2021)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR8" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e474">8</a></sup>.</p><p>The long-term storage of mRNA/LNP formulations remains a significant challenge<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 9" title="Oude Blenke, E. et al. The storage and in-use stability of mRNA vaccines and therapeutics: Not A cold case. J. Pharm. Sci. 112, 386–403 (2023)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR9" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e481">9</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 10" title="Cheng, F. et al. Research advances on the stability of mRNA vaccines. Viruses 15, 668 (2023)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR10" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e484">10</a></sup>. mRNAs are inherently susceptible to degradation <i>via</i> in-line hydrolytic cleavage and oxidation<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 11" title="Wayment-Steele, H. K. et al. Theoretical basis for stabilizing messenger RNA through secondary structure design. Nucleic Acids Res 49, 10604–10617 (2021)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR11" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e491">11</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 12" title="Pogocki, D. &amp; Schöneich, C. Chemical stability of nucleic acid–derived drugs. J. Pharm. Sci. 89, 443–456 (2000)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR12" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e494">12</a></sup>. In addition, LNPs and their components can undergo physical and chemical damage during storage. These degradation processes are accelerated by thermal stress<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 13" title="Kim, B. et al. Optimization of storage conditions for lipid nanoparticle-formulated self-replicating RNA vaccines. J. Control. Release 353, 241–253 (2023)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR13" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e498">13</a></sup>. To mitigate these risks, mRNA/LNP formulations are stored at −20 °C or below. The COVID-19 pandemic led to the establishment of cryogenic storage facilities and logistics systems worldwide; however, it is preferable to avoid cryogenic temperatures for the widespread use of sustainable mRNA therapeutics owing to cost considerations.</p><p>Lyophilization is a promising approach for improving the thermostability of mRNA/LNP<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Kim, B. et al. Optimization of storage conditions for lipid nanoparticle-formulated self-replicating RNA vaccines. J. Control. Release 353, 241–253 (2023)." href="#ref-CR13" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e505">13</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Muramatsu, H. et al. Lyophilization provides long-term stability for a lipid nanoparticle-formulated, nucleoside-modified mRNA vaccine. Mol. Ther. 30, 1941–1951 (2022)." href="#ref-CR14" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e505_1">14</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" title="Ai, L. et al. Lyophilized mRNA-lipid nanoparticle vaccines with long-term stability and high antigenicity against SARS-CoV-2. Cell Discov. 9, 9 (2023)." href="#ref-CR15" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e505_2">15</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 16" title="Meulewaeter, S. et al. Continuous freeze-drying of messenger RNA lipid nanoparticles enables storage at higher temperatures. J. Control. Release 357, 149–160 (2023)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR16" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e508">16</a></sup>. Reducing the amount of residual water in the LNP core decreases the risk of mRNA degradation<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 17" title="Arteta, M. Y. et al. Successful reprogramming of cellular protein production through mRNA delivered by functionalized lipid nanoparticles. Proc. Natl Acad. Sci. USA. 115, E3351–E3360 (2018)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR17" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e512">17</a></sup>, but it requires additional complex, lengthy, and high-energy processes. In addition, the nanoparticles can be potentially damaged and physicochemically changed by reconstitution, even in the presence of cryoprotectants.</p><p>There is a growing demand for thermostable mRNA/LNPs that can be stored in liquid form. It is important to overcome the innate instability of mRNA along with another significant hurdle, that is, an unintended addition reaction of lipid impurities with mRNA. Ionizable lipids with tertiary amines generate aldehyde impurities through oxidation and hydrolysis; these impurities covalently bind to mRNA, compromising their integrity and activity during storage<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18" title="Packer, M., Gyawali, D., Yerabolu, R., Schariter, J. &amp; White, P. A novel mechanism for the loss of mRNA activity in lipid nanoparticle delivery systems. Nat. Commun. 12, 6777 (2021)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR18" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e520">18</a></sup>. This mechanism decreases the thermostability of mRNA/LNP systems. The amine head of ionizable lipids is responsible for aldehyde generation; therefore, we hypothesized that the oxidation and hydrolysis of ionizable lipids and the resulting aldehyde generation could be limited by strategically designing the amine structure; this could enhance the long-term stability of mRNA/LNPs at higher temperatures. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study on the control of aldehyde production based on lipid structure.</p><p>In this study, we identified piperidine-based ionizable lipids that maintained mRNA/LNP activity even after refrigerated storage as liquid formulations. This study provides promising insights into enhancing the shelf-life of LNP-based mRNA from the viewpoint of lipid structure.</p></div></div></section><section data-title="Results"><div class="c-article-section" id="Sec2-section"><h2 class="c-article-section__title js-section-title js-c-reading-companion-sections-item" id="Sec2">Results</h2><div class="c-article-section__content" id="Sec2-content"><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec3">Discovery of piperidine lipids</h3><p>A library of 23 ionizable lipids with <i>N</i>-methyl piperidine head groups was designed for mRNA delivery (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig1">1a</a>). In earlier work on siRNA delivery, <i>N</i>-methyl piperidine was identified as one of the promising head groups<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 19" title="Sato, Y. et al. Understanding structure-activity relationships of pH-sensitive cationic lipids facilitates the rational identification of promising lipid nanoparticles for delivering siRNAs in vivo. J. Control. Release 295, 140–152 (2019)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR19" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e548">19</a></sup>. In addition, heterocyclic amine-containing lipids were reported to elicit a strong immune response in mRNA/LNPs<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 20" title="Miao, L. et al. Delivery of mRNA vaccines with heterocyclic lipids increases anti-tumor efficacy by STING-mediated immune cell activation. Nat. Biotechnol. 37, 1174–1185 (2019)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR20" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e552">20</a></sup>. To enhance functional mRNA delivery, a variety of branched structures were introduced into our current lipid library. The intermediate containing the piperidine structure, synthesized from 6-bromo-1-hexanol, was esterified with branched tails to yield piperidine-ionizable lipids. These lipids were named CL15F m-n, where “m” and “n” are the main and side chain lengths, respectively (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig1">1a</a>). The final products were obtained by purification via both reverse- and normal-phase chromatography and identified using NMR and mass spectrometry.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-1" data-title="Structure and efficacy of piperidine-based ionizable lipids."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig1" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 1: Structure and efficacy of piperidine-based ionizable lipids.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs42003-024-06235-0/MediaObjects/42003_2024_6235_Fig1_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-1-desc ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-1-1" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs42003-024-06235-0/MediaObjects/42003_2024_6235_Fig1_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 1: Structure and efficacy of piperidine-based ionizable lipids." loading="lazy" width="685" height="1152"></picture><span class="u-visually-hidden" id="ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-1-1">The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.</span><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0/figures/1" data-track-dest="link:Figure1 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 1" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-1-desc"><p><b>a</b> Synthetic route of branched ionizable lipids with piperidine, CL15F m-n. Chemical structure of the α-branched carboxylic acid used as a hydrophobic tail in this study. <b>b</b> Chemical structures of the previously developed ionizable lipids, CL4F m-n. <b>c</b> Simplistic illustration for LNP formation by mixing lipids and mRNA with a microfluidic device. The lipid molar ratio was fixed at ionizable lipid: cholesterol: DSPC: DMG-PEG<sub>2k</sub> = 50: 38.5: 10: 1.5. <b>d</b> Relative luciferase expression in HEK-293T cells 24 h after incubation with 100 ng/well FLuc mRNA carried in 10 types of CL4F LNPs, 21 types of CL15F LNPs, and Lipofectamine MessengerMAX. <b>e</b> Lipid structure of ALC-0315 (<i>left</i>) and SM-102 (<i>right</i>). <b>f</b> Timeline of the in vivo study to evaluate the potential application of CL15 LNPs for vaccine use (1 μg OVA mRNA per mouse, <i>n</i> = 4 biologically independent mice per group). <b>g</b> Anti-OVA IgG titers were determined by ELISA 3 weeks after a second intramuscular injection. Statistical significance was calculated by one-way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple comparisons test. <b>h</b>, <b>i</b> OVA-specific interferon-γ (IFN-γ) secreting cells were counted by enzyme-linked immunospot assay 1 week after a second intramuscular injection. Data were analyzed using one-way ANOVA with Dunnett’s test for multiple comparison with ALC-0315. Mean ± SD.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>To evaluate the potency of the CL15F lipid library, we compared it with the CL4F lipid library, which we developed previously <sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 21" title="Hashiba, K. et al. Branching ionizable lipids can enhance the stability, fusogenicity, and functional delivery of mRNA. Small Sci. 3, 2200071 (2023)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR21" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e622">21</a></sup> (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig1">1b</a>). LNPs were assembled by vigorously mixing ionizable lipid, cholesterol (Chol), 1,2-distearoyl-<i>sn</i>-glycero-3-phosphocholine (DSPC), and 1,2-dimyristoyl-<i>rac</i>-glycero-3-methoxypolyethylene glycol-2000 (DMG-PEG<sub>2k</sub>) at a fixed molar ratio with mRNA using a microfluidic device (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig1">1c</a>). While LNPs containing CL15F 6-2, which had the lowest total carbon number, aggregated during the formation process owing to poor hydrophobic interactions, all other CL15F lipids were well formulated without any issues. All LNPs were positively charged in a pH-dependent manner. The apparent pKa values were between 6.24–7.15, which is ideal for mRNA delivery (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S1</a>).</p><p>We first evaluated the in vitro functional delivery of firefly luciferase (FLuc) mRNA using CL15F LNPs. Most CL15F LNPs induced more intense bioluminescence than CL4F LNPs in HEK-293T cells (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig1">1d</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S2a</a>). In addition, some CL15F LNPs exhibited an efficacy comparable to that of Lipofectamine MessengerMAX, a reagent specifically optimized for mRNA transfection in vitro. To elucidate structure-activity relationships (SARs), each lipid structure was described by two parameters (total carbon number and symmetry of the tail). The impact of lipid structure on luciferase activity was visualized as a contour plot (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S2b</a>), confirming that ionizable lipids with branching and longer tails can enhance the functional delivery of mRNA.</p><p>To examine the potential application of CL15F for vaccination, mice were immunized intramuscularly with prime and booster doses of ovalbumin (OVA) mRNA carrying LNPs (Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S1</a>). As positive control lipids, we used lipids that have been clinically successful, namely ALC-0315 and SM-102 (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig1">1e</a>). OVA antibody titers were comparable between CL15F, SM-102, and ALC-0315 LNPs (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig1">1f, g</a>). In addition, mice immunized with CL15F LNPs, particularly those derived from lipids with short tails, exhibited significantly stronger antigen-specific cellular responses than mice immunized with SM-102 and ALC-0315 LNPs (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig1">1h, i</a>). Compared with SM-102, CL15F 9-5 led to a 14-fold increase in IFN-γ spots.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec4">CL15F LNPs control the loss of functional delivery of mRNA at refrigeration temperature</h3><p>The storage stability of mRNA/LNP formulations remains a significant challenge, particularly at refrigeration temperatures. We utilized the hEPO reporter system as a simple assay for evaluating the storage stability of mRNA/LNPs<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 22" title="Ripoll, M. et al. An imidazole modified lipid confers enhanced mRNA-LNP stability and strong immunization properties in mice and non-human primates. Biomaterials 286, 121570 (2022)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR22" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e679">22</a></sup>. To compare the in vivo efficacy before and after mRNA/LNP storage, liver-targeted LNPs are desirable as they can induce hEPO levels sufficient for quantitative evaluation. We examined CL15F 12-10 and CL15F 14-12 and compared them with CL4F 8-6, CL4F 10-4, SM-102, and ALC-0315.</p><p>To assess the in vivo efficacy of hEPO mRNA/LNPs, serum hEPO levels were quantified using ELISA following LNP administration at 0.25 mg/kg. hEPO secretion was confirmed in mice treated with fresh mRNA/LNPs, and this efficacy was comparable to that reported earlier<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 23" title="Dilliard, S. A., Cheng, Q. &amp; Siegwart, D. J. On the mechanism of tissue-specific mRNA delivery by selective organ targeting nanoparticles. Proc. Natl Acad. Sci. Usa. 118, e2109256118 (2021)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR23" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e686">23</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 24" title="Tanaka, H. et al. Ready-to-use-type lyophilized lipid nanoparticle formulation for the postencapsulation of messenger RNA. ACS Nano 17, 2588–2601 (2023)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR24" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e689">24</a></sup> (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S3</a>). We then investigated the in vivo efficacy of the LNPs stored at the given temperatures and time periods (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig2">2a</a>). The loss of hEPO expression in mRNA/LNP treatment was limited at -80 °C storage for up to 5 months (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig2">2b</a>); this is consistent with the knowledge that ultra-low temperature storage with cryoprotectant minimizes damage to LNPs and preserves mRNA potency <sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 25" title="Kafetzis, K. N. et al. The effect of cryoprotectants and storage conditions on the transfection efficiency, stability, and safety of lipid-based nanoparticles for mRNA and DNA delivery. Adv. Healthc. Mater. 12, e2203022 (2023)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR25" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e702">25</a></sup>. CL15F LNPs maintained their in vivo activity after 5 months of storage even at 4 °C as a liquid formulation, whereas the activity of other LNPs decreased over time, with a half-life of approximately 2 months under consistent storage conditions (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig2">2c</a>). No in vivo toxicity was observed, including body weight change, and abnormal serum biochemistry parameters and histological scores at the consistent mRNA dose (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S4</a>).</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-2" data-title="Thermostability of mRNA/LNPs influenced by ionizable lipid structure."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig2" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 2: Thermostability of mRNA/LNPs influenced by ionizable lipid structure.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-2-1" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs42003-024-06235-0/MediaObjects/42003_2024_6235_Fig2_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 2: Thermostability of mRNA/LNPs influenced by ionizable lipid structure." loading="lazy" width="685" height="627"></picture><span class="u-visually-hidden" id="ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-2-1">The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.</span><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0/figures/2" data-track-dest="link:Figure2 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 2" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-2-desc"><p><b>a</b> Timeline of the in vivo study to evaluate storage stability of each LNP. In vivo assay was performed after 1-, 3- and 5-month storage of LNPs at each temperature (<i>n</i> = 3 biologically independent mice per group). <b>b</b> Relative hEPO expression (vs. day 0) 6 h after i.v. administration with 0.25 mg/kg hEPO mRNA encapsulated in LNPs. The in vivo efficacy of each mRNA/LNP was maintained for 5 months at −80 °C storage. <b>c</b> CL15F 12-10 and CL15F 14-12 enabled long-term storage of mRNA/LNP at 4 °C. <b>d</b> CAD chromatograms after 1-, 3-, and 5-month storage of CL15F 12-10 LNPs at 4 °C. <b>e</b> CAD chromatograms after 1-, 3-, and 5-month storage of CL15F 14-12 LNPs at 4 °C. Mean ± SD.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>None of the tested LNPs showed obvious changes in their physicochemical properties or mRNA encapsulation (Supplementary Table <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S2</a>). The integrity of each lipid component during storage was confirmed through high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) using a corona-charged aerosol detector (CAD)<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 26" title="Kinsey, C. et al. Determination of lipid content and stability in lipid nanoparticles using ultra high-performance liquid chromatography in combination with a Corona Charged Aerosol Detector. Electrophoresis 43, 1091–1100 (2022)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR26" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e756">26</a></sup> (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig2">2d, e</a>, Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S5</a>).</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec5">Aldehyde impurities are responsible for mRNA deactivation</h3><p>To evaluate the differences in shelf-life between CL15F LNPs and the other LNPs, we focused on the interaction between mRNA and LNPs, as reported by Packer et al.<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18" title="Packer, M., Gyawali, D., Yerabolu, R., Schariter, J. &amp; White, P. A novel mechanism for the loss of mRNA activity in lipid nanoparticle delivery systems. Nat. Commun. 12, 6777 (2021)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR18" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e774">18</a></sup>; oxidative impurities in ionizable lipids, <i>N</i>-oxides, and the resulting hydrophobic aldehyde impurities lead to deactivation of mRNA cargo owing to their potential reactivity with nucleosides.</p><p>To confirm this, we evaluated the relative amount of aldehyde impurities using a simple fluorescence-based microplate assay with 4-hydrazino-7-nitro-2,1,3-benzoxadiazole hydrazine (NBD-H), which reacts with carbonyl compounds and converts them into fluorescent hydrazones<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 27" title="Koizumi, H. &amp; Suzuki, Y. High-performance liquid chromatography of aliphatic aldehydes by means of post-column extraction with fluorometric detection. J. Chromatogr. A 457, 299–307 (1988)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR27" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e784">27</a>,<a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 28" title="Vidal, N. et al. High throughput assay for evaluation of reactive carbonyl scavenging capacity. Redox Biol. 2, 590–598 (2014)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR28" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e787">28</a></sup> (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig3">3a</a>). NBD-H emitted fluorescence only in the presence of aldehyde species under mild conditions (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S6</a>). When ionizable lipids and helper lipids were incubated with NBD-H, the sample with CL15F and helper lipids emitted significantly lower fluorescence signals than the samples with CL4F and other ionizable lipids (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig3">3b</a>). Therefore, ionizable lipids are a major source of aldehyde impurities, whereas CL15F ionizable lipids yield minimal aldehyde species.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-3" data-title="Quantitative and qualitative analysis of aldehyde impurities."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig3" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 3: Quantitative and qualitative analysis of aldehyde impurities.</b></figcaption><div class="c-article-section__figure-content"><div class="c-article-section__figure-item"><picture class="c-article-section__figure-picture"><source type="image/webp" srcset="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs42003-024-06235-0/MediaObjects/42003_2024_6235_Fig3_HTML.png?as=webp"><img aria-describedby="figure-3-desc ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-3-1" src="//media.springernature.com/lw685/springer-static/image/art%3A10.1038%2Fs42003-024-06235-0/MediaObjects/42003_2024_6235_Fig3_HTML.png" alt="Fig. 3: Quantitative and qualitative analysis of aldehyde impurities." loading="lazy" width="685" height="757"></picture><span class="u-visually-hidden" id="ai-alt-disclaimer-figure-3-1">The alternative text for this image may have been generated using AI.</span><div class="c-article-section__figure-link"><a class="c-article__pill-button" data-test="article-link" data-track="click" data-track-label="button" data-track-action="view figure" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0/figures/3" data-track-dest="link:Figure3 Full size image" aria-label="Full size image figure 3" rel="nofollow"><span>Full size image</span><svg width="16" height="16" focusable="false" role="img" aria-hidden="true" class="u-icon"><use xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" xlink:href="#icon-eds-i-chevron-right-small"></use></svg></a></div></div><div class="c-article-section__figure-description" data-test="bottom-caption" id="figure-3-desc"><p><b>a</b> NBD-H reacts with aldehyde impurities in ionizable lipids to emit fluorescence. <b>b</b> The 38 types of ionizable lipids and other lipids were incubated with NBD-H for 60 min at 25 °C. After incubation, fluorescence intensity was compared; almost all CL15F lipids showed a small amount of aldehyde impurities compared to CL4F lipids and other ionizable lipids. Mean ± SD. <b>c</b> (<i>left</i>) DNPH-labeled aldehyde impurities were detected using a UV detector with reversed-phase ultra-high-performance liquid chromatography with QTOF mass spectrometry (UPLC-QTOF-MS); CL4F 10-8 (orange), CL4F 11-5 (red), and cholesterol (black) as the negative control. DNPH-derivatized fatty aldehyde impurities eluted at 6.5 min with CL4F 10-8 and at 6.2 min with CL4F 11-5. (<i>right</i>) MS spectra of each peak in the range m/z 950 − 1050. The MS spectrum from the peak in DNPH-derivatized CL4F 10-8 corresponds to the exact mass (1013.7887) of fatty aldehyde generated from CL4F 10-8 (Upper right). The MS spectrum from the peak in DNPH-derivatized CL4F 11-5 corresponds to the exact mass (957.7261) of fatty aldehyde generated from CL4F 11-5 (Lower right). <b>d</b> The main source of aldehyde byproducts is CL4F lipids via <i>N</i>-oxidation. In particular, fatty aldehydes localize internally in LNPs and react with the mRNA encapsulated in LNPs to inactivate mRNA.</p></div></div></figure></div><p>To identify the chemical structure of the aldehyde impurities originating from ionizable lipids, we performed LC-MS analysis on CL4F lipid samples labeled with 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine hydrochloride (DNPH). Carbonyl compounds reacted with DNPH to form stable hydrazones, which were detected using a UV detector; this enabled the detection of small amounts of aldehyde impurities in the samples. CL4F 10-8 and CL4F 11-5, which exhibited higher values in the NBD-H assay, were selected as samples. The absorbance at 360 nm in the UV chromatogram revealed the presence of DNPH-derivatized carbonyl compounds in each sample (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig3">3c</a>). In addition, mass spectrometry coupled with isotopic patterns allowed us to identify the peak of interest as a fatty aldehyde corresponding to each ionizable lipid with high reliability.</p><p>We assessed whether <i>N</i>-oxidized ionizable lipids are the main drivers of aldehyde production, as indicated by Packer et al.<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 18" title="Packer, M., Gyawali, D., Yerabolu, R., Schariter, J. &amp; White, P. A novel mechanism for the loss of mRNA activity in lipid nanoparticle delivery systems. Nat. Commun. 12, 6777 (2021)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR18" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e853">18</a></sup>. We synthesized <i>N</i>-oxidized CL4F lipids and incubated them in an acidic environment. <i>N</i>-oxidized CL4F lipids generated higher levels of aldehyde impurities than the non-oxidized parent lipids (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S7</a>).</p><p>Therefore, aldehyde impurities were produced from CL4F lipids via <i>N</i>-oxidation (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig3">3d</a>). This finding is supported by the detection of dipropylamine and fatty secondary amines corresponding to <i>N</i>-oxidized CL4F lipids (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S8</a>, <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S9</a>). Among these degradation byproducts, fatty aldehydes possibly play a significant role in the loss of mRNA function, given their ability to localize within the LNP core and react with nucleobases (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S10</a>). The logP values for propionaldehyde and formaldehyde were 0.59 and 0.35, respectively. In the case of Spikevax, the volume ratio of lipids to water is approximately 0.4%. Therefore, most (98.5 ~ 99% or more) of the water-soluble aldehydes generated from <i>N</i>-oxides inside the LNP would possibly be distributed in the outer aqueous phase and be unlikely to react with mRNA.</p><h3 class="c-article__sub-heading" id="Sec6">Adduct formation between aldehyde impurities and mRNA</h3><p>To assess the potential impact of aldehyde impurities on mRNA function, we first investigated their reactivity with nucleosides. CL4F 11-5 ionizable lipids were incubated with each nucleoside, and the samples were analyzed using hydrophilic interaction chromatography (HILIC). The HILIC mode, which is designed to retain small polar compounds using MS-friendly mobile phases, allows easier separation and identification of aldehyde adduct nucleosides<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 29" title="Virgiliou, C., Gika, H. G. &amp; Theodoridis, G. A. HILIC-MS/MS multi-targeted method for metabolomics applications. Methods Mol. Biol. 1738, 65–81 (2018)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR29" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e900">29</a></sup>. A peak prior to each nucleoside peak was detected in the samples incubated with CL4F lipids, except for the guanosine-lipid conjugate (Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="figure anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#Fig4">4a–d</a>). The mass spectra indicate that each earlier peak corresponds to nucleosides modified by fatty aldehydes derived from CL4F 11-5 (Supplementary Fig. <a data-track="click" data-track-label="link" data-track-action="supplementary material anchor" href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#MOESM2">S11</a>). Under these conditions, the relative peak area derived from the adducts varied; this applied to the absence of lipid-guanosine adducts, which could be attributed to the difference in the reactivity and stability depending on the nucleobase type<sup><a data-track="click" data-track-action="reference anchor" data-track-label="link" data-test="citation-ref" aria-label="Reference 30" title="Shishodia, S. et al. NMR analyses on N-hydroxymethylated nucleobases – Implications for formaldehyde toxicity and nucleic acid demethylases. Org. Biomol. Chem. 16, 4021–4032 (2018)." href="/articles/s42003-024-06235-0#ref-CR30" id="ref-link-section-d160476257e910">30</a></sup>.</p><div class="c-article-section__figure js-c-reading-companion-figures-item" data-test="figure" data-container-section="figure" id="figure-4" data-title="Aldehyde impurities causing adduct formation with mRNA."><figure><figcaption><b id="Fig4" class="c-article-section__figure-caption" data-test="figure-caption-text">Fig. 4: Aldehyde impurities causing adduct formation with mRNA.</b></figcaption><div
